It is also **Mercury** <note>original: "Mercurius"; here referring to the philosophical principle of Quicksilver</note> that overcomes the fire and is not overcome by it, but rather rests gracefully within it and rejoices in it. For, due to the good cohesion of its parts in its strong mixture—namely, when its parts are made thick by the fire—it does not allow itself to be further corrupted. Nor does it allow itself to be raised up into a smoke when a fuming flame reaches it, since it does not allow itself to be "killed" <note>In alchemy, to "kill" a metal often meant to change its outward form or oxidize it</note> because of its hardness and incombustibility.

Therefore, you should not put flies or dung beetles into your Work <note>The author warns against using "foul" or common organic waste materials, a common mistake for novice alchemists</note>, and you should also not eat of the son whose mother is shamed; instead, eat a piece of the fatty flesh, because it is a great folly that you would want to make your Work from worse things when it can be made from something better. (124)

Since we cannot express our **Stone** by name in any other way, nor call it by other names, we instead name it to some extent through the description of its root. And this is done for this reason: because the **Stone** is called by the Philosophers <note>original: "Philoſophis"</note> every thing that possesses within itself and from itself everything that is necessary for its own **perfection**.

It is called by all names because of the excellence of its nature, and because of its hidden and diverse **effects**. It is found everywhere because it partakes of all the **elements**. It is the most worthless of all things because of the **putrefaction** <note>The stage of "blackness" or decay in the alchemical process where the matter dies to be reborn.</note>; yet it is the most precious of all because of its power and virtue. It is black, white, and yellow, according to the transformation of its colors.

Therefore, do not be troubled by the multitude of its names; for let it be called whatever one wishes, it is nevertheless always only one single and identical thing.

In truth, the Philosophers do not concern themselves with names, but rather look to the properties of the names, because through the one they give the other to be understood; for a thing is not subject to speech, but rather speech is subject to the thing—that is, speech must be directed toward the property of the thing.

Accordingly, our **Medicine** <note>A common alchemical term for the Philosopher's Stone, implying its power to "heal" or transmute base metals into gold.</note> is only one thing in its essence, and uniform in its effect: but it is nevertheless necessary that this very same Medicine...

must be white before it can become red, because it cannot turn red unless it has first been white. For one cannot arrive from the first stage to the third except through the second. In the same way, there is no path from the Black to the Yellow except through the White; for the Yellow is composed of much White and a little Black.

Therefore, if you do not first bring this <term>Medicine</term><note>The "Medicine" refers to the Philosopher's Stone or the alchemical substance in its transformative state.</note> to Whiteness, you will not be able to produce the true Redness afterward.

Thus, the white and red <term>Matter</term><note>original: "Materia"</note> are not different in their essential being, but only in this: that the red Medicine requires a greater <term>subtilization</term><note>The process of refining a substance to its most volatile or pure form.</note> and a longer boiling and heat of the fire in its <term>governance</term><note>original: "Regierung"; refers to the controlled management of the furnace heat.</note>. And this is because the end of the work toward the White is the beginning of the work toward the Red. And what is finished and fulfilled in the one must be started anew in the other. For the entire masterpiece is begun upon a Whiteness and also finished there. However, the red work must have a proper <term>ferment</term><note>An agent added to the alchemical matter to "leaven" it and trigger the final transformation.</note>, just as the White requires a white one.

(127)

If the white smoke were to escape and fly away, the entire work would come to nothing and possess no power.

The governance <note>The term "governance" (Regierung) refers to the controlled management of the chemical process, particularly the heat.</note> of our Stone, however, is but a single task: namely, that one boils it constantly and perpetually in its vessel until you reach the desired end.

Nevertheless, the Philosophers <note>The "Philosophers" refers to the ancient and medieval alchemists who established the tradition.</note> have established many artistic maneuvers in honor of their art and to conceal it—primarily regarding the purification, so that nothing foul or impure might be added. Such maneuvers are these: mixing, boiling, sublimating, roasting, grinding, hardening or thickening, watering, putrefying, whitening, and reddening. These are indeed many names, but the governance is of only one kind—namely, that one only boils. For this reason, the entire work is nothing other than "women's work" and "child's play." <note>These are common alchemical metaphors suggesting that once the initial preparation is correct, the process requires only patience and gentle heat, rather than strenuous labor.</note>

Therefore, grind, boil, and repeat the same; do not let yourself be vexed by repeating it many times. For if the Philosophers knew that only one decoction and one grinding were enough, they would not have repeated their sayings so often. They did this for the reason that one should always grind and boil the compound without ceasing.

Yet, its entire governance consists in the moderation of the fire; for the distinction of its governance lies in the distinction of the degrees <note>The "degrees" refer to the specific levels of heat applied to the furnace at different stages of the alchemical process.</note>

Gold and silver are made from it when it is turned back into the first *Matter* <note>original: "Materiam"; the primal substance from which all things are formed</note>. And certainly our *Solution* <note>original: "Solutio"; the process of dissolving a solid into a liquid state</note> is nothing else than the body becoming moist again, and the property of *Mercury* manifesting within it, while the salty taste of its sulfur diminishes. It should not, however, become such a water as comes from the clouds, as some fools have believed; for if it became such a water, it would then become dry by force and not by nature—just like salt and alum—and in such a form, when it melted by the fire, it would be transformed into glass. But that is false and incorrect, and therefore so is their undertaking.

Thus, this is our *Solution*: that you marry *Gabricus* <note>A symbolic name for the masculine principle or "Sulfur," often identified with the brother/king</note> with *Beia* <note>A symbolic name for the feminine principle or "Mercury," often identified with the sister/queen</note>. And although *Beia* is a woman, she nevertheless improves *Gabricus*, for the reason that he has come forth from her. Nevertheless, from the revolution of the heavens, both fortunate and unfortunate things emerge here upon the earth. In the same way, our <term>Stone</term><note>The Philosopher's Stone, the ultimate goal of the alchemical process</note> shall always remain enclosed in its vessel until it has drawn its moisture into itself and, being perfectly nourished by the heat of the fire, has become white; for then it is born, and the blowing of the air does no harm to it.

It is therefore highly necessary to persevere steadily with the work, to maintain a moderate fire, to keep out the air, and especially to do so until it has become white.
<margin>-</margin> Therefore, burn our ore with a gentle fire, just as eggs are brooded, until the body is torn apart and the tincture is drawn out. However, it is not drawn out all at once;
<margin>-</margin> rather, a little comes out every day, and then a little more, until in the fullness of time it reaches its completion. And that which is dissolved always rises to the top, although that which remains below is greater in quantity.
   For this reason, always take care that the fire is not too great, so that you do not reach the dissolution before the proper time and before it is necessary; for such a thing would turn you very far away from the right path, and the work would be brought to no effect or motion. For an all too great heat destroys the composite <note>The "composite" refers to the mixture of substances being transformed in the alchemical vessel</note>, a coldness drives it away, but a gentle warmth nourishes and preserves it.

The preparing power always clarifies and divides, just as it whitens and nourishes: For Nature is wise, and has for herself everything of which she has need.

It is indeed her work that she transforms the earth into water, and the water into earth, according to different effects: Because the water first exerts itself to dissolve the earth, so that it may become fine and thin according to its nature: But afterwards, the earth will make the water hard, so that it—just like the earth—can endure the fire: And this is the <term>Solution<note>original: "Solutio"; the process of dissolving a solid into liquid</note></term> of the body, and the <term>Coagulation<note>original: "coagulatio"; the thickening or solidifying of a liquid</note></term> of the spirit, through decoction in a gentle fire over 150 days: And perhaps the Whiteness might well reveal itself in 70 days: But the first way is better, insofar as it signifies a tempered fire and good preparation.

Nevertheless, there is nothing that attacks gold quite like mercury, because it allows itself to be mixed well and is corrosive. But it makes the body itself black, consumes and torments it, because it is of its own nature. However, the reason it makes it black is that it opens the door to the fixed <note>In alchemy, "fixed" refers to substances that are stable in fire and do not evaporate</note>, and transforms the fixed with the volatile: But in that it martyrs and torments it, such happens not with harm or ruin, but with union and utility. For if its torment were harmful and improper, it would not be accepted by it, nor would it extract its colors,

## CHAPTER VIII.

After this, one must constantly persevere with a gentle fire until it has acquired a dark mist. For the opinion of the philosophers <note>original: "Philosophorum"; referring to the "natural philosophers" or alchemists of antiquity</note> is that the body, having been dissolved into a black powder, now enters back into its water and becomes entirely one single thing. But should you ask and say: "What kind of powder will come from water?" then know that this same powder is nothing other than a water of sulfur <note>original: "Schweffels"; sulfur was considered one of the three primary principles of matter</note> which has been dissolved by the heat of the fire. Therefore, by right, this water accepts the other as its own nature. Consequently, if everything is not turned into water, you will in no way reach perfection <note>original: "Perfection"; the completion of the Great Work</note>; for one must absolutely use nothing else in the mixing, in the grinding, and in the entire governance <note>the controlled management of the alchemical furnace</note> than this same permanent water alone. And note that its power is a spiritual blood <note>original: "spiritualisch Geblüte"</note>, without which nothing can be made.

## CHAPTER IX.

Whoever now knows how to marry, to make pregnant, to give birth, to kill, and to make alive, to bring in a light, and further to cleanse it from its blackness <note>original: "Schwärtze," referring to the Nigredo or initial stage of decay in alchemy</note> and darkness, he shall be in high honor. It is vital that you decompose both the tinting agent <note>original: "tingirende"; the substance that imparts color or transformative power</note> and that which is to be tinted. it was breathed into them from their moisture, just as man receives life from the air, whereby he multiplies and grows according to his kind, as do all other things.

Thus, one vapor rightly holds the other, for the reason that both are composed together at the same time during the <term>decoction<note>original: "Kochung" - the process of heating a substance in a liquid to extract its essence or mature it.</note></term>. Therefore, the volatile nature—even though its essence is nothing but volatile—has nevertheless ceased to be volatile, because in the <term>sublimation<note>The process where a solid is heated into a vapor and then condensed back into a solid, used here to purify the substance.</note></term> one has been joined to the other.

For this reason, the entire <term>Work<note>The "Great Work" or "Opus Magnum," referring to the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</note></term> should be frequently sublimated into a vapor over a moderate fire, so that it may be breathed upon by the air and be able to live. For the life of all natural things consists of the breathing-in of the air. Therefore, the entire Work also consists in the vapor and sublimation of its water.

But you must take the greatest care in every sublimation that your glass or your vessel is not broken. For if you were to kindle the fire so strongly that the water had to rise to the uppermost part of the vessel, it would rejoice in the cooling and cling there; and thus you would not be able to perform the sublimation of the <term>elements<note>The four basic constituents: Earth, Air, Fire, and Water.</note></term>. The reason is that each among them must be brought down and raised up again for itself many times through <term>circulation<note>A process where vapors rise, condense, and flow back down to the bottom of the vessel in a continuous cycle.</note></term>. But that which rises up by force does not come down again without force.

(146)

Therefore, your fire should be gentle, so that the entire work may rise and fall freely of its own accord, without sticking to the vessel. For this reason, unless we make the body thin with water and fire until it rises as a spirit <note>original: "Spiritus"; refers to the volatile essence of a substance</note>, we achieve nothing.

But when it rises into the air, it is born and transformed into air, and becomes a life such that one cannot be separated from the other—just as one cannot separate water that has been mixed with other water. Therefore, that which is born in the air is born whitish, since it is born entirely and completely spiritual.

Now take note that we perform the sublimation <note>The process of heating a substance until it turns into vapor and then solidifies again, used here to purify the material</note> for three reasons: The first is so that the body may become a spirit of a subtle nature. The second is so that the spirit may incorporate itself with the body and become one with it. The third is so that everything may become pure and white, and the saltiness of the sulfur <note>original: "Schweffel"; in alchemy, sulfur represents the active, combustible principle of a substance</note> may be diminished; for through sublimation, whatever is combustible in the work burns away.

And in truth, it is highly necessary for us that the elements <note>original: "Elementa"</note> become simple, so that they may be composed together; yet they cannot become simple if they are not separated into their parts. Therefore, one must frequently sublimate both vapors until the water descends in a transparent state.

We sift the substances through the round, spherical sieve seven times, so that everything becomes a bright, clear water; for the body <note>The "body" refers to the solid, material residue of the alchemical substance.</note> never allows the soul to be separated from it, as the soul is like the body in simple affinity. Therefore, we repeat their sublimation <note>Sublimation: the process of heating a solid until it turns into vapor and then solidifies again, used here to purify the spirit from the dross.</note> so that they may be transformed into a subtle nature.

Accordingly, one must drive the separation with all one's power, so that nothing of the fatness of the soul remains in the body, except for that which is not noticed during its elevation; otherwise, the process will be hindered in its effect.

For it is sifted through the sieve of the round sphere, one after another, and the body settles to the bottom, while the water rises up white with the simple, kindred soul.

So, always pour one part together with the other, so that when they are rectified <note>Rectified: purified or refined through repeated distillation.</note> they can withstand the elements, and especially the fire. For whatever does not withstand that which opposes it will be cast aside and diminish. Therefore, the body must be dry and parched, the soul moist, and the spirit flowing.

Some believe that the separation happens in many different ways, but you should not heed them; for in this you will fare well: that you bring the work to a simple equality, and not by having each element separated individually.

Nevertheless, she—as a pious mother—cherishes, tends, and nourishes me, her son whom she has begotten, as best she can until I am able to reach a state of perfection.

# The Fifth Part

Once the toad <note>The "Toad" is a common alchemical symbol for the black mass or "nigredo" stage of the Great Work, representing the base matter before purification.</note> has been devoured, it will become white both inside and out.

You must bring the water back upon the earth and govern it with a moderate fire until it has taken root in its own nature. Know for certain that it must first be nourished with a little milk, just as one sees in the raising of a child. For with a little milk it is nourished and warmed by a small fire as long as it is small. And the more it increases, the more food and warmth it requires, until it has soaked up its own moisture. For the first moisture is cold; therefore, one must guard against too great a fire, which is contrary to the cold.

However, if you place the body upon the fire without vinegar <note>original: "Eßig"; in alchemy, "vinegar" often refers to a specialized solvent or the "mercurial water" used to dissolve the solid matter.</note>, it will be burnt, and that which is sought after will not come from it. For when the fire encounters no moisture that it can dry out, the body burns. But the vinegar that is added to it prevents the burning of the fire and dries itself along with the body so that it suffers no harm. And the more it is burdened with the flame of the fire, the more it hides itself in the innermost part of the water, so that it does not burn from the heat of the fire.

But it is not my will that you should pour all the water on at once, lest the Elixir <term>Elixir: the transformative substance or "Philosopher's Stone" in its liquid or powdered form</term> be drowned. Instead, pour the water on gradually, and the body should always be with the third <insert>part of the water</insert>...

Part of its water must be boiled away. For if it were made without proper weight <note>meaning the correct proportions or balance of elements</note>, death would come to it; and where that happens, nothing good can arise from it. But where it is governed in a fitting manner, it is pacified with its water over the fire.

Because the body initially has little root, the power of which is not equally great in all parts—just as the sun's radiance is not equal in all the planets—patience and a long duration are necessary, so that through the length of the boiling, the water may overcome the conflict of the fire. For through gentle boiling, the water becomes hard, and the corrupting moisture is drawn out; the heat of the radical moisture <term>radical moisture: the essential, life-sustaining fluid believed to exist within all matter</term> is increased, and the dryness is preserved from burning.

<margin><unclear>Δ</unclear> <note>This symbol likely represents Fire or a specific stage of the process</note></margin>
It may well happen that one work becomes hard sooner and another more slowly, which necessarily results from the difference in the boiling. For if the place where it is boiled is moist and dewy, the work will be finished all the sooner; but where it is dry, it will harden more slowly.

<margin>|</margin>
Therefore, I command that our fire always be gentle until the water becomes stone and remains at the bottom. For within forty days, it will be entirely transformed into earth.

<margin><unclear>p-</unclear></margin>
Therefore, when you see that the water hardens by itself, then be assured that your science <note>original: "Wissenschafft," referring here to the alchemical art or knowledge</note> is correct. For the body...

## CHAPTER XII.

The water, however, must be divided into two parts, of which the first is coagulated <note>term: coagulation; the process of turning a liquid into a solid or semi-solid state</note> into the body, and the other becomes rotten and passes away. To coagulate means to turn a watery substance into an earth; but to putrefy <note>term: putrefaction; the decomposition or "rotting" of the matter, seen as a necessary stage of transformation</note> is the same as to resolve <note>term: resolution; the breaking down of a solid back into a liquid state</note> that which was coagulated.
For putrefaction does not happen except in the moist and the dry together; for in this way the spirit <note>original: "Spiritus"; the volatile or gaseous essence of the matter</note> will penetrate into the body, and there will be a mixing through and through.
The fire, however, which is kept beneath it, must be gentle. Also, beware that you do not take it out of its vessel too quickly, otherwise it might die; for no exit is open to a little child <note>A common alchemical metaphor for the developing "Philosopher's Stone" or the refined substance</note> until it is able to draw breath from the air.
Therefore, keep it always closed up, with

# CHAPTER XIII.

Therefore, take the volatile <note>original: "Flüchtige," referring to substances that evaporate easily, often symbolized by birds</note> with the flying, sink it down, and make it subject to you, so that it may be obedient to you and no longer fly in the airs, but with its taking flight, go no further than your pleasure allows. For that which is here below is like that which is above, and what is above is just like that which is below <note>A reference to the famous Hermetic maxim from the Emerald Tablet: "As above, so below."</note>. Therefore, let our blessed water come down to moisten its earths, to cleanse the blackness <term>Nigredo: the initial stage of the alchemical process characterized by decomposition and darkness</term>, and to take away all evil stench, because there is a desire between them, like that between man and woman.

Therefore, take care that its moisture does not escape from the vessel and perish, but bring the same back to the earth, and coagulate <note>to thicken or solidify a liquid</note> it with gentle fire, just as the seed is coagulated in the mother: For the woman, when she has embraced her husband, it goes quickly into her body. For that reason, bring the water back over its earths until it is coagulated on top, for then it transitions all the sooner from one nature into the other, and in every degree of its operation, it takes on a new property.

Give the ashes their sweat back again; grind, cook, repeat, and do not let yourself be vexed by doing such things often: For the earth does not turn green without much moistening; it also takes the moist—

# CHAPTER XV.

Now observe how the <term>compound<note>original: "Compositum"; the mixture of substances prepared for the alchemical work</note></term> drinks in the water, and how its color changes at every stage. For when the matter is placed upon the fire, it alters its colors many times, shifting from one to another through the operation of nature.

However, the spirit and the soul are not united with the body except in the white color. For as long as the dark blackness shows itself, the woman <note>In alchemy, the "woman" or "feminine principle" often represents the cold, moist, and receptive element, associated with the initial stage of decay.</note> reigns; and she is indeed the first power of our <term>Stone<note>The Philosopher's Stone</note></term>, because if it does not become black, it can never become white or red. For the redness is composed of black and white together. Furthermore, when a tempered heat works upon the moisture, it produces the blackness, dries out the humidity, and removes the corruption.

On the contrary, if the heat becomes too strong, it causes the redness <insert>prematurely</insert>, drives away the moisture, and brings about corruption.

From this it is evident that where the compound is driven more than is proper, it is immediately spoiled and cannot become that which is sought and desired. Therefore, one must take great care that the water is not turned into smoke by too strong a fire and flee away. For this reason, the struggle between water and fire should be of long duration.

of boiling / for just as water is dried out by the sun's warmth / in the same manner, through the gentle boiling of the fire, the water / which is coagulated <note>Coagulation: the process of turning a liquid into a solid or semi-solid state, a key stage in the Great Work.</note> in the earth / can hold itself against the struggle of the fire.

Accordingly, the water shall overcome the conflict of the fire through tedious boiling / thickening and coagulating itself. For if you perfectly knew its nature / you would certainly endure the length of the boiling with pleasure / until you reach your purpose / since the goodness of this venerable nature in the bodies is highly praised / for this reason / because it absolutely does not allow itself to be driven away / nor divided into the parts from which it is composed / but either retreats from the fire with its entire substance / or remains entirely constant in the fire / therefore a cause of perfection is necessarily to be perceived in it.

Therefore, govern this nature patiently / gently coagulate the mercury <note>original: "Quecksilber"</note> / which rains down upon it from our ore. Bitter clouds rise up / and the rain falls upon the earth / for every dense and heavy body <note>original: "Corpus"</note> falls naturally toward the center. But the mercury which is sublimated <note>Sublimation: the process of heating a substance until it turns into vapor and then solidifies again, used here to purify the matter.</note> from the ore / from which everything arises / is a pure water / and the true *tincture* <note>Tincture: the elusive "medicine" or substance capable of transforming base metals into gold or silver.</note> / which wipes out the shadow of the ore: For it is the white *sulfur* <note>original: "Sulphur"</note> / which alone makes the ore white / and through which the *spirit* <note>original: "Spiritus"</note> is held—

### CHAPTER XVI.

Honor, therefore, the King and his Wife <note>The King and Queen are common alchemical symbols for Sulfur and Mercury, or the solar and polar opposites being joined</note>, and do not burn them, lest they flee from you because of the great heat; for you have need of patience and long-suffering, which improve the King and his consort in their governance. Cook them until they become black, thereafter white, thereafter red, and then it will be a medicine that tints into gold.

   Inasmuch as our ore, the more it is cooked at the beginning, the more it is dissolved <note>original: "solviret"</note> and becomes a spiritual water. Thereafter, the more it is cooked, the thicker it becomes, and it becomes a very white powder. Thirdly, the more it is cooked, the more it is colored, and it becomes a tincture of high redness; and this entire work is nothing other than the extraction of the water from the earth, and the pouring of the same back upon the earth. But there must be diligent attendance; there must be a continuous hope: for everything will explain itself, and thus finally your desire will come to pass.

   So now let your water, which is thus entirely extracted and found within the earth, remain for several days over—

The common man putrefies by moisture alone; but our putrefaction does not happen without the moist and the dry together, because the moisture is kept solely within the dry so that it cannot escape.

Since heavy things cannot be raised up without the presence of light things, nor can light things be brought down without the company of heavy things; and both are the beginning and the end of the Work.

Thus, the colors will now surely teach you how you should behave regarding the fire; since they will indicate to you at what time and for how long you should maintain the fire of the first, second, and third degree <note>Alchemists categorized heat into degrees, ranging from a gentle "lukewarm" heat to intense, direct bellows-fire</note>. Therefore, if you are a diligent laboratory worker <note>original: "Laborant"</note>, the colors will certainly teach you what must be done.

For at all times in the dissolution <note>original: "Solution"</note> and coagulation <note>original: "Coagulatio"</note> the fire should be gentle; in the sublimation <note>The process of turning a solid into vapor and back to solid to purify it</note> it should be moderate; and in the reddening <note>original: "Rohtmachung," the final stage of the Great Work</note> it should be strong.

But yet, the more the colors transform, the more one must persist with gentle fire during the whitening <note>The "Albedo" stage of alchemy</note>, until it reaches the end of the white color. For before the whiteness, we do not give it much fire, for the reason that it is of a cold nature, raw and half-cooked; and therefore the white governs until the white color is achieved.

For this reason, the fire should be gentle during the whitening, until the vapor becomes one form with its body <note>original: "Cörper"</note>. Otherwise, if you kindle the fire too much, it will become red before the appointed time, which is of no use; because the red color is composed of much white and little black with great heat. Hence these rhymes:

But the dry principle hardly accepts an impression, and hardly lets it go again.

Therefore, when the moist and the dry have been tempered <note>balanced or blended</note> with one another, the dry easily receives an image from the moist, and the moist obtains from the dry the ability to hold that image firmly and withstand all fire. Thus, for the sake of the moisture, the dry is prevented from separating; and for the sake of the dry, the moist is prevented from being able to flow any longer.

An example of this is the work of a potter, who binds the earth with water and cooks it by the fire, so that one does not let go of the other.

In this way, create your masterpiece <note>the *Magnum Opus* or Great Work of alchemy</note> and rule the earth only with moisture and warmth, until they unite and become equal, and are no longer contrary to each other, nor separate. ## CHAPTER XVIII.

Thus you have now, dear friend, learned to make the white work; now you must also know how to prepare the red. But yet, if you do not make it white, you will also not be able to make the true red, just as no one can come from the first to the third except through the second. In the same manner, you cannot come from the black to the yellow without passing through the white, because the yellow is composed of much white and little black. Therefore, make the black white, and make the white red, and you shall have the masterpiece.

If the urine is white, and we know that we have slept little, we return again to bed: but when we have slept further, the digestion is fully completed, and the urine becomes yellow.

In the same manner, solely through the cooking <note>original: "Kochung" - a standard alchemical term for the application of steady heat over time</note> after the white stage, you will arrive at the redness; since you cannot err if you continue to persist with a dry fire. You surely see that the seed is not sired from blood unless it is first diligently cooked in the liver until it receives a deep red color; and if that does not happen, nothing will be sired from the seed. In the same way, our white ore <note>original: "Erz" - likely referring to the 'White Stone' or 'White Elixir' stage of the Great Work</note>, if it is not diligently cooked, it will not become red. And certainly, the warmth of the liver alone makes the digested juice <note>original: "Safft" - refers to the chyle or bodily humors</note> red, just as the ignited heat of the fire alone makes the white sulfur <term>Sulphur: in alchemy, the active, masculine principle of combustibility and soul</term> red. For the first digestion in the stomach makes everything white, but the second in the liver makes everything red; for in the liver alone is the warmth powerful, just as in the stomach the dryness is powerful.

Therefore, with dry fire and dry calcination <term>Calcination: the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to drive off volatile matter or reduce it to powder</term>, cook the dry substance until it becomes red like cinnabar <note>original: "Zinnober" - a bright red mineral (mercury sulfide) used here as a color benchmark</note>; to which you must by no means add water, nor anything else, until it becomes completely red through cooking. For just as in summer when the fruits are ripe, much rain is harmful: so too does too much moisture—

## CHAPTER XX.

The quality of this multiplication comes from nowhere else but from the manifold repeated sublimation and fixation of this Medicine: For the more often the order of this work is repeated, the more abundantly it is increased, and its power and perfection become so great that it transforms all imperfect bodies—yes, even quicksilver <note>original: "Quecksilber"</note>—innumerably into true gold and silver. For as often as you sublimate and dissolve the perfect Medicine, you will gain every time in the projection <note>The final stage of alchemy where the medicine is cast upon base metals to transform them.</note> by adding one part to a thousand parts: So that if it first falls upon a thousand parts, it can the second time be applied to ten thousand, the third to a hundred thousand, the fourth to a thousand times a thousand, and the fifth to innumerable parts.

<note>The author emphasizes that the potency of the philosopher's stone increases exponentially through repeated cycles of refinement.</note>

<meta>Continuity established with the catchword "For" from the previous page, discussing the refinement of the alchemical substance.</meta>

Therefore, the more subtle the **Elixir** <note>A medicinal or alchemical preparation capable of prolonging life or transmuting metals.</note> is made, the more it **tinctures** <note>original: "tingiret"; the process of staining or imparting a new quality/color to a metal.</note>; and indeed, the more you **sublimate** <note>original: "sublimiren"; to purify a substance by heating it into vapor and then cooling it back into a solid.</note> your work, the more power it will possess, so that it will act more abundantly and transform a greater quantity.

And that is exactly what **Geber** <note>Jabir ibn Hayyan (c. 721–815), a legendary alchemist often called the "Father of Chemistry."</note> says, who was a master over all masters in our art:

> Go to work with your **Stone** and with my **Stone**, and my Stone shall have more power than yours: For if you cast yours upon a thousand, I will cast mine upon ten thousand, and all shall be well. But if you cast upon ten thousand, I will cast one part upon a thousand times a thousand. Therefore, my Stone must be more powerful than yours, since it surpasses your Stone in the transformation of many thousands.

However, Geber spoke this to mislead us, for his Stone and our Stone are one and the same; but he **dissolved** <note>original: "solvirte"; the process of breaking down a solid into a liquid state.</note> the whole and sublimated it many times, so that one part of his Stone surpassed ten of ours. And therefore, the more it is sublimated, the greater power of **transformation** it will have; and thus, that which is sublimated the most also possesses the greatest **virtue** <note>original: "Tugend"; here meaning inherent power or medicinal efficacy.</note>. For this reason, you must not cease in making your Elixir subtle, and in sublimating it as much as you possibly can. And certainly, the cause of its rapid **melting** is the highest refinement of its parts by the fire.

<summary>This page discusses the necessity of repeated sublimation to increase the potency of the Alchemical Elixir, citing the authority of Geber to illustrate how refinement dictates the scale of transmutation. It clarifies that the "Master's Stone" is not a different substance, but simply a more highly purified version of the common Stone.</summary>

For whatever is brought to a state of stability <note>original: "Fixigkeit"; refers to the chemical property of resisting change or evaporation by fire</note> from Mercury in its own nature, that is also more easily dissolved; and therefore, whatever is most dissolved is also made most subtle.

And this is the reason why the completed Elixir <note>The "Philosopher's Stone" in liquid or powder form, capable of transmuting metals</note> allows itself to be poured as easily as wax, more so than any other metal. For it is nothing other than a very pure substance of quicksilver <note>mercury</note> brought to stability, and made of a very pure, subtle, and stable matter which has its origin in quicksilver and is created from the same.

And for this reason—because quicksilver is very easily volatile even without being ignited—it requires another Medicine <note>The transformative agent or catalyst in the alchemical process</note> which, immediately before the quicksilver flees away, clings to it fundamentally and becomes united with it through and through. This Medicine makes it thick and, through its own stability, preserves the quicksilver from fleeing when in the fire; by its power, it transforms it in an instant into true silver and gold, according to how the Elixir has been prepared.

However, if this Medicine were not easily melted, it could not dissolve before the Mercury fled, and thus would not hold it. Furthermore, if it were not of the same nature as the Mercury, it would not cling to it fundamentally and would not allow itself to be mixed through and through. For quicksilver accepts nothing into itself except that which is of its own nature. And if the Medicine were not stable, it would not bring about stability <note>original: "figiren"; to fix or make a volatile substance solid in the fire</note>; and if it were not very pure and shin-

And this is the reason why the Medicine can be multiplied without end. For it is like a fire in wood, and like musk <note>original: "Bisem"</note> among fine spices, whose scent spreads the more it is rubbed. Therefore, you must always keep a portion of it back, because you have been enriched by it, just like one who has fire is so rich that he can share it with others.

The **Medicine** <note>The "Medicine" refers to the Philosopher's Stone or the transformative substance in its final stages.</note> is increased either through **Solution** <note>The process of dissolving a solid into a liquid state.</note> or through **Fermentation**. It increases more slowly through **Solution**; on the other hand, our **Medicine** is increased more quickly through **Fermentation**. This is because that which is **dissolved** <note>original: "solvirte"</note> cannot be easily increased unless it is first made **fixed** <note>Stable and resistant to heat.</note> within its **Ferment**.

However, the **Medicine** works much more abundantly when it is both **dissolved** and **fermented** than when it is **fermented** alone, since it has then become more subtle—although it can also be increased infinitely through **Fermentation** alone. This is because the **Ferment** entirely brings whatever is added to it into its own nature, color, and taste. For it makes the prepared substance white, prevents combustion, and retains the **Tincture** <note>The concentrated essence or color-giving power of the Stone.</note> so that it does not flee away. It makes the bodies living and causes them to come together and penetrate one another, which is the end of the Work; without this, the **Elixir** is not completed, just as a dough is not leavened without sourdough.

Therefore, when you have **sublimated** the **Elixir** and made it **fixed**, then bring its own **Spirit** <note>original: "Spiritum"; the volatile or gaseous essence of the substance.</note> upon it, so that it may be mixed and melted with it through the secret of nature in the **Philosophical Vessel** <note>The specialized glass flask, often called an alembic or retort, used by alchemists.</note>. For when they stand for a time, the stronger among them overcomes the other and transforms the whole into a **Spirit** that is equal to itself; and because it is master of the bonds, it does not remain still <note>Likely referring to the active, transformative state of the volatile spirit before it is re-fixed.</note>.

Certainly, the **Soul** <note>original: "Seele"; the animating essence of the matter</note> returns more readily into its own **Body** <note>original: "Leib"; the solid or fixed base</note> than if it were sent into another. If you undertake to do otherwise, your labor will be in vain; since darkness has no communion with the light.

For they return again to the body which is like those from which they were drawn; for the **Tincture** <note>original: "tingirende"; the substance capable of coloring or transforming other metals</note> and that which is to be transformed have together become one **Tincture**.

## CHAPTER XXI.

Thus you shall now arrange your <term>projection</term> <note>the final stage of alchemy where the elixir is cast upon base metal to transform it</note> in this way: First <term>multiply</term> 10 by 10 / so it will be 100 / and 100 by 100 / that will be 1000 / and 1000 by 1000 / so it will be ten thousand / and ten thousand by 10,000.

(194)
S

From this, one hundred thousand is made; and 100,000 into 100,000, so it becomes a thousand times a thousand; and a thousand times a thousand into 1,000,000, so it becomes an innumerable number.

This is as much as to say: First, project one part onto 10 parts, and one of those ten onto a hundred, so ten thousand will come of it, and so on.

Again, according to the opinion of another philosopher: Project one part onto ten parts of a <term>prepared metal</term> <note>original: "præparirten Metalls"; likely referring to purified base metals like lead or mercury intended for transmutation</note>, and from this again one part onto a hundred; thus it will be transformed into a fixed and permanent transmutation, if it pleases God.

But if you were to cast one part onto a thousand parts all at once and at the very start, it is to be feared that it might be consumed and vanish in smoke due to the excessive heat before it has entered and been completed.

However, when you project one part onto ten parts, it soon penetrates and mixes itself; therefore it is not necessary to strengthen the fire, nor to maintain it for long, but it should soon be set aside. When it has become cold, one part holds to the other and clings to it because of the equality of their nature.

Therefore, when you henceforth cast one part onto a hundred parts, the <term>substance of the stone</term> <note>original: "Substanz des Steins"; the Philosopher's Stone or the completed Elixir</note> is held by that with which it is mixed, and clings to it until it transforms it entirely.

<summary>This page describes the process of "multiplication," explaining how to incrementally increase the power of the alchemical medicine by projecting it onto base metals in specific ratios to ensure stability and prevent loss through excessive heat.</summary>
<keywords>multiplication, projection, transmutation, prepared metal, substance of the stone, alchemical ratios, heat management</keywords>

--- Page 207 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded with instructions on the preparation of the mercury; this page continues with the mixing process and the philosophical justification for the elixir's potency.</meta>

So now mix your **prepared mercury** <note>original: "præparirtes Quecksilber"</note> with oil; not because it is a **material** <note>original: "Materia"</note> of all metals, as some claim and many believe it to be; but rather because, on account of its coldness, it can hold the **medicine** <note>The "medicine" refers to the alchemical catalyst or Elixir</note> over the fire until it mixes and does not evaporate.

Therefore, guard this well, and you shall be happy and prosperous here in this world.

The cause of all this, however, is threefold: namely, **Goodness**, **Necessity**, and **Perfection**. **Goodness** is that it may **tinge** <note>term: "tingire" (to tinge); the alchemical process of spreading a color or quality through a substance to transform it</note> more abundantly, that it may make things more perfect, and that it may transform more.

**Necessity** is that it may give better color, that it may bring forth its own kind, and that it may be all the better **fixed** <note>term: "figiret" (fixed); making a volatile substance stable under heat</note>.

**Perfection** is because the perfect medicine can convert an imperfect metal into perfect gold and silver.

The number of perfection, however, is ten, a hundred, and a thousand: therefore you shall perform **projection** <note>term: "projection"; the final stage of alchemy where the elixir is cast upon molten base metal to transmute it</note> from the first to the last.

For where such a small part of lesser things should strive against so large a part, it would be overcome by the same on account of its smallness.

All the **foundations** of the medicine, however, must be very subtle and tinging; for the more the **elixir** is made subtle and tinged, the more abundantly it works; therefore, that which is most subtle also works the most.

<summary>This page explains the practical and philosophical reasons for mixing mercury with oil to stabilize the alchemical medicine, emphasizing the qualities of goodness, necessity, and perfection. It describes the "projection" process where the refined elixir transmutes base metals into gold and silver through its subtle and "tinging" power.</summary>

<keywords>mercury, alchemy, elixir, projection, transmutation, tincture, fixation, perfection, gold and silver</keywords>

<header>❧(196)☙</header>

Therefore, place one solution <note>original: "Solution"; the liquid state of the material</note> with the other, and let that which is dissolved become dry again. Place it entirely upon the fire, protect it from smoke, and take care that nothing of it escapes.

<margin>-</margin>The entire governance, however, consists in the moderation of the fire. For that reason, remain always by the vessel and behold the wonders: namely, how it moves from one color into another, often many times within a single hour, until it reaches the end <margin>-</margin>of the Whiteness and the Redness <note>The "Albedo" and "Rubedo," the final two stages of the alchemical process</note>, since it will very soon melt in the fire and become hard again in the air.

For when the smoke <note>The volatile vapors</note> feels the fire, it will penetrate into the body, and the spirit <note>original: "Spiritus"; the volatile essence</note> will be bound into the dryness, and will thus become a body <note>original: "Corpus"</note>: fixed, clear, white or red, according to what the medicine and the ferment <note>original: "Fermentum"; the substance that triggers the transformation</note> shall be.

Then remove it from the fire and let it cool completely; for one part of its weight can be cast upon a thousand times a thousand <note>one million</note> and more of any body, which it shall entirely transform into the best gold and silver, according to how the elixir has been prepared.

Nevertheless, the white medicine requires a white ferment, just as the red requires a red one. Since nothing should enter into the red work except the red, just as nothing enters the white except the white.

Therefore, as you do with the white, so also do with the red; for they have

Both have only one and the same preparation; but it is the **ferment** <note>In alchemy, the 'ferment' is the agent—often compared to leaven in bread—that transforms the base matter into the desired elixir.</note> through which the **tinctures** and the **spirits** are joined together.

From this it is clear: he who does not join the **white mercury**—which can withstand the fire—with the clear and pure **mercury** <note>original: "Mercurio"</note>, has not yet chosen the path to the **Whiteness** <note>The 'Albedo' or white stage of the Great Work.</note>. But he who does not join the **red mercury**—which can endure the fire—to the clear, pure **gold**, has not yet selected a path to the **Redness** <note>The 'Rubedo' or final red stage of the Great Work.</note>.

Therefore, he should not weary his body in those things which he will not be able to attain, because he will be of no use to himself, nor to another, nor to the world.

## CHAPTER XXII.

<margin><image-desc>A vertical curved hand-drawn bracket marks the start of this chapter in the right margin.</image-desc></margin>
Because, however, far too lengthy a speech overwhelms the understanding and creates more error, I will describe the entire completion of the **Work** <term>The Great Work (Magnum Opus): the process of creating the Philosopher's Stone</term> in few words. It is conducted in this way: one takes the **Stone**, which is well enough known, and through steady, persistent labor, **sublimes** <note>Sublimation: heating a substance until it vaporizes and then collects as a solid again, used here for purification.</note> it continually until the Stone is first purified and becomes subtle, reaching the highest subtlety and purity, and finally becomes volatile. And this is indeed the first degree of its **governance** <note>original: "Regierung," referring to the controlled stages of the chemical process.</note>.

Afterward, however, it must be made **fixed** <note>Fixation: the process of making a volatile substance stable under heat.</note> according to the manner of **shaping**...

be made / until it can rest in a strong fire.

However, the work for silver should be very white / but the work for gold should be very red.

For the white is a work of the winter and the red is a work of the summer: Therefore it requires a further <term>sublimation</term><note>A process of heating a substance until it vaporizes and then collecting the purified solid deposit.</note> of its parts / according to its own manner / ripened with greater decoction / until it assumes an exceedingly red color. And in this alone consists the goal of the perfection of the second <term>degree</term><note>Latin: gradus; referring to the stages of the alchemical process.</note>.

But in the third degree is the fulfillment of the entire work / and it is this / namely that you make the stone—which has now been made <term>fixed</term><note>Stable and resistant to heat.</note>—volatile again after the manner of sublimation with the <term>unfixed</term><note>Volatile or unstable.</note> / and make the volatile fixed again / and dissolve the fixed once more and make it volatile / and again the volatile fixed / until it flows and melts / and can certainly transform into permanent gold and silver.

From the repeated preparation of this third degree arises the quality of the <term>medicine</term><note>The "Philosopher's Stone" or "Elixir," viewed here as a universal curative.</note> / and the multiplication of the transformation / so that it may infinitely transform each of the <term>imperfect bodies</term><note>Base metals like lead, tin, or copper.</note> into true gold and silver.

Furthermore, it also possesses, above all other medicines, the powerful virtue to cure all illnesses: Inasmuch as it gladdens the spirit / increases strength / preserves youth / and renews old age / since it does not [let] the blood <note>The text cuts off here; the catchword "lässe" suggests the next page begins with "lässe," likely meaning "does not let the blood [spoil/stagnate]."</note>

It neither allows the body to decay, nor permits the <term>Phlegma</term> <note>Tough mucus or phlegm, one of the four classical humors</note> to dominate, nor the yellow bile to burn, nor the <term>black bile</term> <note>Melancholy</note> to take the upper hand. Indeed, it increases the blood supply vigorously; it cleanses whatever sits in the air passages and preserves all members of the body. In short, it cures both hot and cold infirmities better than all other medicines.

For if the sickness has lasted for one month, it heals the same in a single day. If it has lasted for a year, it heals it in twelve days. But if it is older and has persisted for a long time, it cures the same in one month, drives out all evil fluids, and introduces good <term>humors</term> <note>The vital bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—thought to determine health and temperament</note>. It also causes those to whom it is given to be loved; it grants undaunted courage, boldness, and victory to those who carry it with them.

Thus is completed the greatest secret of all the secrets of nature, which is the most precious treasure above all treasures of this

# The Vision of the same John Dastin, Englishman.
<note>John Dastin (c. 1293–1386) was a famous English alchemist who corresponded with Pope John XXII; he argued that alchemy was a natural process rather than magic.</note>

Because all things that exist in Nature have a certain effect of birth and multiplication, we take from their root a necessary significance of the planets. Therefore, as we sought the origin of gold and silver with great desire, we saw such a vision.

It seemed to us as if we were carried away before the face of Nature; and behold, seven planets came at the command of Nature and made their firstborn brother king over them. And when they had placed the crown of the kingdom upon him, they worshipped him and gave him to understand their defects and infirmities: for they were almost all poisoned either with leprosy or with scabs and itch.
<note>In alchemical allegory, "leprosy" and "scabs" refer to the impurities and oxidation found in base metals like lead or iron, which prevent them from being "healthy" or "noble" like gold.</note>

The King, however, wished to comfort his fellow brothers and spoke: "It is certainly very good that one of you, who is without blemish, should die for all the others, so that the whole race does not perish, and so that we, being anointed with his fresh blood, may attain health."
<note>The "blood" of the king is a common metaphor for the "Red Elixir" or the Philosopher's Stone, which was believed to "heal" base metals into gold.</note>

For, as Plato says: according to the merit of the materials, the forms are given.
<note>original: "secundum meritum materiarum dantur formae." This refers to the Aristotelian/Platonic idea that the quality of the physical substance determines what kind of soul or "form" it can hold.</note>

<summary>This page recounts an allegorical vision by the alchemist John Dastin, where the seven planets (representing metals) crown a king and seek a sacrificial cure for their "leprosy" or impurities. It establishes the philosophical foundation for the transmutation of metals through the metaphor of healing and sacrifice.</summary>

<keywords>John Dastin, alchemy, planetary metals, allegory of the king, transmutation, purification, Plato, history of science</keywords>

But the other planets, when they had heard their king, spoke: "Who among us is truly without blemish? For we were conceived from impure seed, and our mother bore us so that we are subject to frailty <note>The "frailty" refers to the tendency of base metals to corrode or oxidize, unlike gold.</note>: Who can now make that pure which is conceived from impure seed? For truly, nothing can be found in a thing that is not already within it."

To these, their mother, <term>Mercury</term> <note>In alchemy, Mercury is often depicted as the "mother" or the fundamental matter of all metals.</note>, answered: "Dear sons, I have fathered six bodies, among which one is shining, unblemished, a king and a head, and the best among the planets; there is nothing superfluous in him, nor too little, for his <term>complexion</term> <note>The balance of the four primary qualities: hot, cold, moist, and dry.</note> is tempered, and his nature is balanced in heat and cold, in moisture and dryness."

"Therefore, burning things do not burn him, nor do corrupting things destroy him; neither earth nor water can ever change him. For this reason, the spirits are mixed with him and are made <term>fixed</term> <note>To "fix" a substance means to make a volatile spirit solid and resistant to heat.</note> through him with great understanding, which is not to be found with a stubborn <term>artist</term> <note>The "artist" refers to the alchemist practicing the Great Work.</note>."

"So we could all indeed become perfect together through him; but because he is perfect in his nature, he will surely not die." Then they all wept bitterly and spoke: "Alas! Mother, why did you father us so impurely, but him so perfectly? Woe unto us! Why..."

"Have you made us brothers of misery and heartache? Why were we not suffocated in our mother's womb, or otherwise perished before we were born? Why were we taken upon the lap and suckled at the breast when we were first born, only to burn and be food for the fire?"

To these, Nature answered thus: "All things are created with wisdom and right measure; he who has given the counsel shall also provide the help. For a good prince lays down his life for his subjects. Therefore, O King, you must be born anew, because otherwise you cannot die, nor can you make your brothers perfect <margin>Note Well</margin> and cure them. *For he who turns the fourth into the sixth, can transform it into the seventh, and into the fourth.*" <note>This numerical sequence likely refers to the stages of alchemical transmutation or the relationship between the elements and the planets.</note>

# Albertus Magnus’s Compendium.
### Or
## A Brief Concept of the Origin and Matter of Metals.

<image-desc>The page begins with a decorative banner composed of two horizontal rows of stylized fleur-de-lis and vine ornaments. A large, ornate woodcut drop cap 'D' initiates the main body of the text, decorated with intricate floral and scrollwork patterns.</image-desc>

The first and most principal matter of all metals in their <term>mines (ore-pits)</term> <note>original: "Mineris (Ertzgruben)"</note> from which they have their origin, is a dry water, which we call the living water or <term>Quicksilver</term> <note>original: "Argentum vivum," the Latin term for mercury</note>: And a stinking spirit, which we otherwise call <term>Sulphur</term>: Yet these are not in their common form as they are created in their mines; for in the places where they are generated, no finished metal is found. Rather, this matter is a substance created by nature that possesses the properties and essence of both of those aforementioned: And from such matter or substance, derived from the two previously mentioned, there is generated and created a subtle essence like a smoke in the veins of the earth and mineral passages, where they gather and are contained.

And thereafter, when the natural power [prevails] over

<summary>This page introduces the alchemical theories of Albertus Magnus regarding the formation of metals, identifying their primary components as "dry water" (mercury) and a "stinking spirit" (sulfur) that combine as a vapor within the earth.</summary>
<keywords>Albertus Magnus, alchemy, origin of metals, mercury, sulfur, quicksilver, mineral veins, geological formation</keywords>

--- Page 224 ---
the unnatural matter arrives, it makes the same smaller and harder, and unifies it with itself in a firm, inseparable union through the natural cooking <note>The process of "cooking" or "coction" refers to the alchemical belief that metals ripen or mature within the earth through steady, natural heat.</note>.

From this it can be seen that the four elements are in the metals in a natural way, and that they are of the same essence, since they are nothing other than very subtle vapors which, through natural cooking in their ores or veins, have coagulated, taken shape, and been transformed into metallic substance.

Likewise, it is to be understood from this that the fundamental moisture of the metals cannot be consumed or separated from them during their calcination <note>Calcination is the process of heating a substance to high temperatures in order to drive off volatile substances or achieve oxidation.</note> because of their uniformity and the strong union of the elements with one another, just as the moisture of stone is separated; for in them, it is united with great dryness.

## The manner of Nature in the birthing of metals is very wonderful.

There is in the ores a uniform matter, namely <term>Argentum vivum</term> <note>original: "Argentum vivum"; literally "living silver," the historical term for Quicksilver or Mercury.</note> and sulfur in one body. From this matter, Art <note>In this context, "Art" refers to Alchemy or the human imitation of natural processes.</note> easily and naturally causes a misty essence or subtle matter to arise, which through the help of Nature is transformed into a metal. a *Body* <note>original: "Corpus"</note> and the vapor generated from it is a *Spirit* <note>original: "Spiritus"</note>. Thus Nature makes a *Spirit* out of the body: and so causes it to ascend from the Earth into the Heaven; that is, she makes the corporeal thing spiritual, and thereafter, through the Circulation, she makes the spiritual thing bodily again, so that it descends from the Heaven back into the Earth.

It is also to be seen from this, that all metals—as concerns the root of Nature—are of one and the same substance or matter: and yet they are not of one and the same figure or form. And this happens because of the weakness and strength, purity and impurity, scarcity or abundance of the substance of the Quicksilver <note>Mercury</note> and Sulfur in their natural union and composition: and because of the differing properties of the minerals, and also the longer or shorter ripening or *decoction* <term>Decoction: a process of "cooking" or maturing substances through steady heat</term> of Nature herself.

The **Earth** and the **Air** are the passive elements. **Water** and **Fire** are the active ones. **Water** is the husband; **Earth** is the wife.

## A Missive
### **BY JOHANNES PONTANUS.**

**I**, Johannes Pontanus <note>A celebrated 15th-century humanist and scholar, though this text is attributed to him in the context of the "Sophic Fire."</note>, have traveled through many lands so that I might learn something certain regarding the Philosopher's Stone <note>The legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold and granting long life.</note>; and though I have wandered through nearly the entire world, I encountered many false deceivers and no true *philosophers* <note>original: "Philosophos"; here meaning true alchemists who understand nature.</note>. However, I studied diligently at all times, and after manifold doubts, I finally arrived upon the path of truth.

But after the matter <note>The "materia prima" or starting substance of the alchemical work.</note> became somewhat known to me, I nevertheless erred many times before I discovered the correct matter, its actual effect, and how it was to be handled.

Initially, I set to work with the matter by putting it through the process of putrefaction <note>The breaking down or "rotting" of the substance to release its essence.</note> for nine months, but I found nothing. I also kept it in a *water bath* <note>original: "Balneo Mar." or Balneum Mariae; a gentle heating method using hot water.</note> for a period of time, and likewise erred. After that, I kept it in a calcining fire <note>A high-heat furnace used to reduce substances to ash or powder.</note> for three months, and that was also not correctly managed. I attempted and tried all types of *distillation* and *sublimation* <note>Processes of vaporizing and condensing substances to purify them.</note> (as the *philosophers* speak, or seem to speak, such as *Geber* <note>Jabir ibn Hayyan, the father of Arabic chemistry.</note>, *Archelaus*, and almost all others); but I found nothing. Finally, I sought the *subject* <note>The underlying physical substance or "subjectum" of the work.</note> of the entire art of alchemy in every way that one could imagine, and which

<summary>Johannes Pontanus recounts his extensive travels and numerous failed laboratory experiments—including putrefaction and distillation—in his long search for the true secret of the Philosopher's Stone.</summary>
<keywords>Johannes Pontanus, Philosopher's Stone, alchemy, putrefaction, water bath, calcination, Geber, Archelaus, distillation, sublimation</keywords>

--- Page 234 ---
<meta>The previous page likely discussed the author's initial failed experiments with traditional heating methods. The catchword "fel" from the previous page connects to the beginning of this page's first word "verrichtet" (performed).</meta>

which are performed by means of manure, the *water bath* <note>original: "Balneum"; a vessel of hot water used for gentle, even heating</note>, ashes, and other fires of many kinds. Although these are found in the books of the philosophers, I have attempted to prepare them, but I have found nothing of value.

Therefore, for three years together, I have diligently read the books of the philosophers, especially *Hermes* <note>referring to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure to whom the foundational texts of alchemy are attributed</note> alone, whose brief words contain within them the entire science of the Stone; although he speaks in dark riddles concerning the Upper and the Lower, and of Heaven and Earth.

Thus, our instrument, in which the matter is brought into its true essence during the first, second, and third work, is not a fire of the *water bath*, nor of horse manure, nor of ashes, nor of any other kind that the wise men have set down in their books.

What kind of fire is it then, which performs the entire work from beginning to end? Certainly, the *philosophers* <note>original: "Philosophi"; here meaning the alchemists of old</note> have kept the same secret and concealed.

But I, moved by true-hearted love, wish to explain this to you, as well as the preparation of the entire work.

Now, our Stone is only one thing, but many names are given to it; therefore, it will be difficult for you before you learn to recognize it.

For it is of a watery, airy, fiery, and earthy nature: it is phlegmatic, choleric, and melancholic <note>these refer to the four humors or temperaments believed to govern all physical matter</note>: it is sulfurous—

Those who read Geber <note>original: "Gebrum"; referring to Jabir ibn Hayyan, a famous 8th-century alchemist</note> and all other <term>philosophers</term> <note>In this context, "philosophers" refers specifically to alchemists</note>, even if they lived a hundred thousand years, they would still not grasp it; for this fire is discovered through deep reflection alone, and then—but not before—can it be understood in their books.

( 225 )

have not established the effective method, except for one who is called Artephius <note>Artephius was a legendary 12th-century alchemist, often claimed to have lived for a thousand years by using the "Elixir of Life."</note>; yet even he speaks for himself. And if I had not read Artephius and taken note of his speeches, I would never have arrived at the completion of the Work.

The preparation of the Work is this: One should take the Stone and grind it as diligently as possible according to the natural way, and then let it come to the fire. However, one must know the proportion or equality of the fire—namely, that the material should only be encouraged <note>The author warns against using excessive heat, suggesting a gentle "nurturing" fire instead.</note>. Then, in a short time and without any manual labor, that same fire will certainly complete the entire Work. For it will <term>putrefy</term> <note>original: "putreficiren"; the process of breaking down or "rotting" the matter to allow for new growth</note>, destroy, regenerate, and bring it to perfection. It will cause the three principal colors to appear: namely Black, White, and Red <note>These represent the three main stages of the alchemical Great Work: Nigredo, Albedo, and Rubedo.</note>.

Furthermore, by means of our fire, the Medicine can be increased when it is joined together with the raw or first material—not only in quantity or size, but also in power and effect.

Accordingly, you should seek to investigate your fire with all your strength, and you will surely succeed; for it performs the entire Work and is the key of all the Sages, which they have never discovered or revealed.

But if you [follow] the aforementioned words regarding the...

--- Page 238 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded with a discussion on the nature of fire and its role in the alchemical process; this page continues that instruction, emphasizing the distinction between the fire and the material it acts upon.</meta>

If you will reflect well and deeply upon the properties of **Fire**, it will become well known to you; otherwise, it shall not.

I have written this now, moved by love and faithfulness; but so that I may fully satisfy your inquiry, you should know that the **Fire** is not changed by the **Matter**, for it does not originate from the **Matter**, as I have mentioned above. <note>The author emphasizes that the alchemical "fire"—often interpreted as a spiritual or catalytic force—remains pure and distinct from the physical substances being transformed.</note>

I have wished to indicate this, and to remind those who are wise, so that they do not waste their money uselessly, but may instead know what they ought to seek. In such a way—and no other—will they arrive at the **Truth of the Art**. <note>The "Art" refers to Alchemy, specifically the Hermetic tradition of transmutation.</note>

Be commended to **God**.

If you cannot see the truth within this Book of Art,
And the Work will not succeed according to your mind,
<margin>Hand</margin>
Then I faithfully advise you: withdraw your hand soon,
Otherwise your money and property will be spent in vain.

# The Entire Art Briefly Composed

I advise you to take that which Nature has given,
And place it in a glass; seal it very tightly;
Moisten it with moist fire <note>A reference to the "balneum Mariae" or water bath, providing gentle, consistent heat</note>, as the Sages teach you,
Then it will finally become that which your heart desires.

(228)

Put it in a vat /
And make it wet /
Then from that, this will beget.
S. Tell me then! What?
M. A noble treasure /
That beyond measure
Keeps body and blood
In health and good.
In this world wide
Nothing stands beside;
It brings great wealth /
And by its stealth /
The metals all /
Lead, copper, steel /
To gold it turns.
Whatever one yearns /
One can possess /
Through this largesse.
For its power and truth
Preserve one's youth /
The graying hair
Falls out right there <note>The text suggests that gray hair falls out to be replaced by hair of its original youthful color.</note>;
Sickness it drives away /
It will not stay.

--- Page 242 ---
<meta>The previous page likely discussed the difficulty of understanding alchemical secrets; this page addresses the student's frustration with the lack of clear instructions.</meta>

(230)

You complain that the Art <note>The "Art" refers to Alchemy or the Great Work of transformation.</note> is not described clearly,
And how one might go by a straight path to the Work.
For an answer, take this: In no book can one find
How to begin so that one might conceive a child.
And yet the work is so very easily accomplished,
That even a peasant lad, who has never seen it,
Can perform it. Yes, indeed. But you say:
More belongs to the dance than a pair of new shoes;
There must also be a pair of straight legs,
The floor must be level, not full of sharp stones.
But here I do not find what one should rightly take
For the beginning of this Art, so that it may succeed.
Also, the Philosophers <note>The "Philosophers" are the ancient alchemical authorities like Hermes Trismegistus or Geber.</note> often lay great stones in the way
With unknown words, which then move a person
And lead them now here, now onto another path,
So that a thorough understanding can be grasped nowhere.
To this, have this report: The Sages all write,
God reveals this to the one who pleases Him.

<summary>The author addresses the student's complaint that alchemical texts are intentionally obscure, comparing the natural ease of the "Work" to procreation while acknowledging the confusing terminology used by ancient philosophers.</summary>
<keywords>Alchemy, The Great Work, Divine Revelation, Philosophical Obscurity, Metaphor of Procreation, Spiritual Worthiness</keywords>

( 231 )

Him you must call upon, so that He may enlighten
your understanding; otherwise, the Work will remain unknown
to you.
Also, the Philosophers <note>original: "Philosophi"; referring here to the ancient alchemists and sages</note> are by no means to be hated,
for they have left behind good accounts in their writings,
and as much as God allowed them to reveal
of this Masterpiece <note>original: "Meisterstück"; the Magnum Opus or the Philosopher's Stone</note>, that they have
done.
Therefore, if you wish for the Art <note>The "Art" refers to Alchemy or the Hermetic science</note> to succeed for you,
then pray to God, that He may bring you onto the right path:
And follow Nature; also consider her weariness <note>original: "Müaligkeit"; likely referring to the natural processes of decay and regeneration, or the limitations of the physical world</note>
rightly therein. Herewith you have your instruction.
